Python parent spawns detached 'python3 -' child reading payload from stdin AIInstallation High
Detects the stage-1 fileless downloader pattern in spellcheckpy: a Python interpreter is spawned by another Python process and invoked with the sole '-' argument (read script from stdin), receiving the RAT body through the parent's pipe and surviving via start_new_session=True. Nothing touches disk. Rationale: Verbatim lift from the article's subprocess.Popen(["python3","-"], stdin=subprocess.PIPE, start_new_session=True) snippet. Python-spawns-Python with a sole '-' argument is rare outside of test rigs and uniquely matches the stage-1 RAT loader. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/malicious-pypi-packages-spellcheckpy-and-spellcheckerpy-deliver-python-rat
ATT&CKT1059.006 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer running 'cat script.py | python -' interactively in a shell
  • CI build step piping a generated script into a fresh python interpreter (some setuptools custom commands)
  • Jupyter kernel restart edge cases where the kernel relauncher uses '-'
  • Fuzz / property-test harness piping generated scripts into python for crash detection
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessAccountName in~ ("sec-research","malware-analyst") and InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "sandbox") | where AccountName !endswith "$"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): pivot on DeviceName + LogonId to find both parent and detached child; inspect the parent's InitiatingProcessFolderPath — site-packages/spellchecker* or any folder containing resources/eu.json.gz is a confirmation. If EDR captured stdin contents, dump them. Step 2 (contain): kill the detached child (start_new_session=True orphans it from the original parent — use AuthenticationId/LogonId to locate by session, not by ppid), isolate the host, block updatenet.work + 172.86.73.139 + dothebest.store. Step 3 (hunt): pivot to UC3 for that host's outbound to the C2, look back 30d for sibling python pids matching this stdin-pipe pattern, and sweep all hosts for site-packages/spellchecker* presence.

C2 beacon or stage-2 fetch to updatenet[.]work / 172.86.73.139 / dothebest[.]store AICommand & Control Medium
Detects outbound HTTP(S) or DNS traffic to the spellcheckpy C2 domain updatenet.work, the RouterHosting/Cloudzy A-record 172.86.73.139, or the prior-campaign C2 dothebest.store (HelixGuard report). The RAT beacons /update1.php every 5 seconds — a single hit on this triplet is high-fidelity. Rationale: Three IOCs named directly by the article: updatenet.work (live C2 + /update1.php beacon + /settings/history.php stage-2), 172.86.73.139 (RouterHosting/Cloudzy A-record), and dothebest.store (prior-campaign C2 from HelixGuard write-up that the article links the same actor to). Each is unique enough that a single hit is investigatable; ContextImageFileName == python.exe with site-packages/spellchecker* in path is the confirmation pivot. Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): identify the originating host and ContextImageFileName / InitiatingProcessFileName — python.exe with InitiatingProcessFolderPath containing 'site-packages\\spellchecker' or 'site-packages/spellchecker' is a confirmed match. Capture the 5-second beacon cadence (4-byte command-ID + 4-byte length + XOR-encrypted body, outer key 03 06 02 01 06 00 04 07 00 01 09 06 08 01 02 05, inner key 0x7B). Step 2 (contain): isolate host via MDE live response, drop 172.86.73.139 and the entire 172.86.73.0/24 RouterHosting subnet plus updatenet.work and dothebest.store at the perimeter; kill all python processes on the host whose folder path contains 'spellchecker'. Step 3 (hunt): sweep every host org-wide for traffic to the IOC triplet over the prior 90d, pivot on TLS JA3 from the beacon if recorded, pull all AS14956 RouterHosting flows for the same window, look for resources/eu.json.gz files containing the base64 'spellchecker' key on disk, and search PyPI install history for campaign

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

ATT&CKT1059.001 T1027

DeviceProcessEvents

| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

status: experimental
description: |

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files

| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",

                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")

          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName

| where Account !endswith "$"

        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high
ENDPOINT

       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents

| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process

          FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,

CISA KEV: CVE-2026-20045 — Cisco Unified Communications Products Code Injection Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-01-21 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Cisco Unified Communications Manager (Unified CM), Cisco Unified Communications Manager Session Management Edition (Unified CM SME), Cisco Unified Communications Manager IM & Presence Service (Unified CM IM&P), Cisco Unity Connection, and Cisco Webex Calling Dedicated Instance contain a code injection vulnerability that could allow the attacker to obtain user-level access to the underlying operating system and then elevate privileges to root. Vendor: Cisco, Product: Unified Communications Manager. Federal patch due: 2026-02-11.

// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine

ServiceNow's Virtual Agent Vulnerability Shows Why AI Security Needs Traditional AppSec Foundations

2026-01-14 6 use cases 2 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

The critical ServiceNow Virtual Agent vulnerability highlights a vital lesson: securing agentic AI requires a return to traditional AppSec foundations. While AI can amplify risks, the root causes often stem from classic failures in authentication and authorization.

BodySnatcher (CVE-2025-12420) — Hardcoded 'servicenowexternalagent' Token Observed in HTTP Traffic AIExploitation High
Hunts HTTP requests carrying the hardcoded shared token string 'servicenowexternalagent' that AppOmni documented as the static credential used by every third-party integration to authenticate to the ServiceNow Virtual Agent API. Any inbound request bearing this token to a Now-platform host indicates exploitation of CVE-2025-12420 (BodySnatcher) since the credential is identical across all customer tenants. Rationale: Anchored on the literal credential string 'servicenowexternalagent' published verbatim in code-block 1 of the Snyk article (and corroborated by AppOmni's advisory). The token is identical across every ServiceNow tenant, so any occurrence in WAF, web-proxy, IIS, or EDR network telemetry is a high-fidelity exploitation signal for CVE-2025-12420. Cross-checked against: • https://www.appomni.com/blog/servicenow-virtual-agent-vulnerability • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-12420 • https://www.servicenow.com/community/now-platform-articles/security-advisory-cve-2025-12420/
ATT&CKT1078.001 T1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal red-team / purple-team replay of the AppOmni PoC against staging ServiceNow tenants
  • Vendor vulnerability scanners (Qualys, Tenable, Rapid7) executing the AppOmni signature check
  • Security researchers / journalists hosting the IOC string on educational pages indexed by web-proxy logs
  • AppOmni / ServiceNow remediation-verification probes during patch deployment windows
  • SIEM ingest of historical PoC reproduction documents stored in SharePoint / Confluence (false positive when CommonSecurityLog captures the URL of a wiki page containing the string)
Suppression filter
// Suppress vendor scanners and internal red-team test ranges that we know legitimately probe this IOC.
| where SourceIP !in ("<qualys_scanner_cidr>", "<tenable_scanner_cidr>", "<internal_redteam_cidr>")
| where not (UA has_any ("Qualys", "Nessus", "Tenable", "Rapid7", "AppOmni"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Confirm the destination host is a ServiceNow Now-platform instance (`*.service-now.com`). Pull the full HTTP request — Authorization header, JSON body, source IP — and validate the token appears verbatim ('servicenowexternalagent', no rotation suffix). Cross-check whether the request originated from a known integration partner subnet or an unexpected external IP. Step 2 (Contain): Engage ServiceNow admin to rotate the OAuth credential for the affected integration, disable the Virtual Agent API channel for unauthenticated third-party callers, and apply the November-2025 patch if not already deployed. Block the source IP at the WAF if external. Step 3 (Hunt): Query ServiceNow sys_audit / syslog for any sys_user record created in the last 30d with admin role attached, plus any data created/modified by the 'AIA-Agent Invoker' user in the same window. Pivot to UC #2 (AIA-Agent Invoker AutoChat Topic invocation) on the same source IPs.
// Hunt the hardcoded 'servicenowexternalagent' token across any HTTP context Defender sees (URL clicks, network events with URL captured).
let IOCToken = "servicenowexternalagent";
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has IOCToken or AdditionalFields has IOCToken
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| union (UrlClickEvents | where Timestamp > ago(30d) | where Url has IOCToken | project Timestamp, AccountUpn, Url, NetworkMessageId, IPAddress)
| order by Timestamp desc
// Hunt the hardcoded 'servicenowexternalagent' token across WAF, ASIM web, and IIS telemetry.
let IOCToken = "servicenowexternalagent";
let WAFHits = CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where RequestURL has IOCToken
        or RequestClientApplication has IOCToken
        or RequestCookies has IOCToken
        or RequestContext has IOCToken
        or Message has IOCToken
        or DeviceCustomString1 has IOCToken
        or DeviceCustomString2 has IOCToken
        or DeviceCustomString3 has IOCToken
        or DeviceCustomString4 has IOCToken
        or DeviceCustomString5 has IOCToken
        or DeviceCustomString6 has IOCToken
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="WAF", SourceIP=SourceIP, DestinationHost=DestinationHostName, URL=RequestURL, UA=RequestClientApplication, Action=DeviceAction;
let WebHits = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where Url has IOCToken
        or UrlOriginal has IOCToken
        or HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization has IOCToken
        or HttpRequestHeaderCookie has IOCToken
        or HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent has IOCToken
        or HttpRequestHeaderReferer has IOCToken
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="ASIM-Web", SourceIP=SrcIpAddr, DestinationHost=HttpHostName, URL=Url, UA=HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, Action=DvcAction;
let IISHits = W3CIISLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where csUriQuery has IOCToken
        or csUriStem has IOCToken
        or csUserAgent has IOCToken
        or csReferer has IOCToken
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="IIS", SourceIP=cIP, DestinationHost=Computer, URL=strcat(csUriStem, "?", csUriQuery), UA=csUserAgent, Action=tostring(scStatus);
union WAFHits, WebHits, IISHits
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: Detects HTTP requests containing the hardcoded shared credential 'servicenowexternalagent' that ServiceNow shipped identically across every customer tenant for third-party Virtual Agent API integrations (CVE-2025-12420 / BodySnatcher).
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/servicenow-virtual-agent-vulnerability/

author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/01/14

  selection_ua:
    cs(User-Agent)|contains: 'servicenowexternalagent'
  condition: 1 of selection_*
falsepositives:

  - Vendor remediation scanners probing for the issue

ServiceNow Virtual Agent Invocation of Hidden AIA-Agent Invoker AutoChat Topic (CVE-2025-12420) AIExploitation High
Hunts unauthenticated or weakly-authenticated invocations of the hidden ServiceNow AutoChat topic that wires the 'Record Management AI Agent' to the Virtual Agent API surface. AppOmni's PoC showed the BodySnatcher chain pivots through this internal topic once the static 'servicenowexternalagent' token is replayed, so any external client hitting the chatbot/v1/openframe or /api/now/v1/awa endpoints with an email-only identity payload referencing 'Record Management' is high-fidelity for CVE-2025-12420 exploitation. Rationale: Anchored on the two literal strings in code-block 1 ('servicenowexternalagent') and code-block 2 ('Record Management AI Agent') from the Snyk article, plus the AppOmni-disclosed AutoChat / AIA-Agent Invoker pivot path. The combination of a Now-platform host, a Virtual Agent API path, and one of the agent markers is uniquely tied to BodySnatcher exploitation — legitimate chatbot traffic from end users won't carry the 'Record Management' agent name in the request body because that agent is admin-only. Cross-checked against: • https://www.appomni.com/blog/servicenow-virtual-agent-vulnerability • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-12420 • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1190/
ATT&CKT1190 T1078.004 T1136.003
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate ServiceNow integration partners (UiPath, Microsoft Copilot Studio, Zendesk) calling the Virtual Agent API with their own scoped OAuth tokens
  • ServiceNow administrators testing the Record Management AI Agent from the platform's own admin UI during patch validation
  • Internal HR/IT self-service portals that embed the Virtual Agent chatbot widget and reference agent names in request bodies
  • AppOmni / Snyk vulnerability scanners specifically probing for CVE-2025-12420
  • ServiceNow's own MID-Server health checks that exercise the AWA API endpoint
Suppression filter
// Allow-list known ServiceNow integration partner egress IPs and the platform's own MID-Server ranges.
| where SourceIP !in ("<approved_integration_partner_cidrs>", "<servicenow_mid_server_cidr>")
| where not (UA has_any ("ServiceNow-MID", "AppOmni-Scanner", "Snyk-Vuln-Scanner"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Pull the full HTTP request payload from the WAF / proxy log. Verify it carries an email-only identity claim (no MFA, no SSO assertion) and that the body references 'Record Management' or 'AIA-Agent Invoker'. Cross-correlate the source IP against your inventory of approved ServiceNow integration partners. Step 2 (Contain): Have the ServiceNow admin disable the Virtual Agent third-party API channel, rotate any 'servicenowexternalagent'-style shared credentials, and apply ServiceNow's CVE-2025-12420 hotfix. Block the source IP at the WAF. Step 3 (Hunt): Query the ServiceNow sys_audit table for any sys_user records created or modified by 'AIA-Agent Invoker' in the last 30 days. Enumerate which records the impersonated mailbox can now access in downstream-connected systems (Salesforce, M365) — assume lateral movement until proven otherwise. Force password reset on any admin accounts created post-incident.
// Detect outbound calls from any device to ServiceNow tenants invoking the Virtual Agent / AutoChat API while carrying Record-Management agent indicators (CVE-2025-12420 BodySnatcher).
let SnowHost = "service-now.com";
let ChatAPI = dynamic(["/api/now/v1/awa", "/api/now/cs/", "/api/sn_va_as_service", "virtualagent", "openframe"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has SnowHost
| where RemoteUrl has_any (ChatAPI)
| where AdditionalFields has_any ("Record Management", "AIA-Agent Invoker", "servicenowexternalagent", "sys_user")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountUpn, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, AdditionalFields
| order by Timestamp desc
// Detect ServiceNow Virtual Agent / AutoChat API calls that invoke the Record-Management AI Agent (CVE-2025-12420 BodySnatcher).
let SnowHost = "service-now.com";
let ChatPaths = dynamic(["/api/now/v1/awa", "/api/now/cs/", "/api/sn_va_as_service", "virtualagent", "openframe"]);
let AgentMarkers = dynamic(["Record Management", "AIA-Agent Invoker", "servicenowexternalagent", "AutoChat"]);
let WAFHits = CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where DestinationHostName has SnowHost or RequestURL has SnowHost
    | where RequestURL has_any (ChatPaths)
    | where RequestURL has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or RequestCookies has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or RequestContext has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or Message has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or DeviceCustomString1 has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or DeviceCustomString2 has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or DeviceCustomString3 has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or DeviceCustomString4 has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or DeviceCustomString5 has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or DeviceCustomString6 has_any (AgentMarkers)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="WAF", SourceIP, DestinationHost=DestinationHostName, Method=RequestMethod, URL=RequestURL, UA=RequestClientApplication, Status=tostring(EventOutcome);
let WebHits = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where HttpHostName has SnowHost or Url has SnowHost
    | where Url has_any (ChatPaths) or UrlOriginal has_any (ChatPaths)
    | where Url has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or UrlOriginal has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or HttpRequestHeaderCookie has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or HttpRequestHeaderReferer has_any (AgentMarkers)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="ASIM-Web", SourceIP=SrcIpAddr, DestinationHost=HttpHostName, Method=HttpMethod, URL=Url, UA=HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, Status=tostring(HttpStatusCode);
let IISHits = W3CIISLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where csUriStem has_any (ChatPaths)
    | where csUriQuery has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or csUriStem has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or csUserAgent has_any (AgentMarkers)
        or csReferer has_any (AgentMarkers)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="IIS", SourceIP=cIP, DestinationHost=Computer, Method=csMethod, URL=strcat(csUriStem, "?", csUriQuery), UA=csUserAgent, Status=tostring(scStatus);
union WAFHits, WebHits, IISHits
| order by TimeGenerated desc
(source:cloudtrail OR source:azure.activity_logs) (@http.url:*service-now.com* AND (@http.url:*/api/now/v1/awa* OR @http.url:*/api/now/cs/* OR @http.url:*virtualagent* OR @http.url:*sn_va_as_service*) AND (@http.request.body:(*"Record Management"* OR *"AIA-Agent Invoker"* OR *servicenowexternalagent* OR *AutoChat*) OR @http.url:*servicenowexternalagent*))
#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/service-now\.com$/i
| join({#event_simpleName=NetworkConnectIP4}, field=[aid, ContextProcessId])
| join({#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 | regex("(Record Management|AIA-Agent Invoker|servicenowexternalagent|AutoChat)", field=CommandLine, ignoreCase=true)}, field=[aid, ContextProcessId])
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, DomainName, CommandLine], function=count())
WEB

description: |

    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02

    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'

tj-actions/changed-files compromised commit SHA referenced in workflow YAML or git history AIWeaponization High
Hunts for references to the known-compromised tj-actions/changed-files commit SHA (0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67, March 2025) in process command lines that touch workflow YAML or perform git/checkout operations on developer or CI-runner endpoints. The compromised action dumped CI/CD secrets to workflow logs, so any post-incident reference to that SHA in a repo is grounds for secret rotation. Rationale: The compromised commit SHA 0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67 is the canonical Indicator referenced across StepSecurity, GitHub Security Advisory, and CISA write-ups of the tj-actions/changed-files March 2025 compromise — any process cmdline still referencing it indicates a workflow has not been remediated or a developer is reproducing a vulnerable build. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/2025-in-review-the-evolution-of-supply-chain-security-whats-next • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/002/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1078
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researchers/red teamers replaying the IOC in a lab repo
  • Internal postmortem/training materials checked into the same repo containing the SHA in markdown
  • Forensic timeline scripts grep-ing repos for the SHA as part of remediation tooling
  • Existing detection-engineering content (Sigma rules, this rule itself) referencing the SHA when run from a code-editor host
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("code.exe", "devenv.exe", "sublime_text.exe") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("grep", "rg", "findstr"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: identify the user and repo; check whether the cmdline is `git checkout`, a CI Runner pulling the action, or a grep/forensic tool. Inspect the parent process (text editor / CI agent / shell) to gauge intent. Step 2 — Contain: if the host is a self-hosted GitHub runner or developer workstation that actually executed this version of the action, rotate every secret exposed to the affected workflow run (PATs, cloud keys, npm tokens) and disable any OIDC trust relationships for the workflow. Pin the repo to a SHA, not @v45, going forward. Step 3 — Hunt: search GitHub audit log for `workflows.completed_workflow_run` referencing the action in the last 90 days; pull workflow logs for any run that ran a pinned-version-on-or-before the compromise window; cross-check against AWS CloudTrail/Azure AAD for use of any of those secrets from anomalous IPs.

Nx s1ngularity-repository creation via GitHub API from developer or CI endpoint AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts for the s1ngularity npm-supply-chain payload exfil pattern from August 2025: malicious nx postinstall scripts use a stolen GitHub token to create public repositories named `s1ngularity-repository[-N]` containing a base64-encoded `results.b64` blob of stolen credentials. Detect either the outbound GitHub API write call or the local results.b64 staging file. Rationale: `results.b64` and `telemetry.js` are the two filenames called out across StepSecurity and the GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c advisory as the s1ngularity payload's exfil-staging and dropper artefacts; pairing them with a Node-family parent suppresses unrelated `results.b64` files (e.g. unit-test fixtures) and isolates the supply chain attack write path. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/2025-in-review-the-evolution-of-supply-chain-security-whats-next • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c
ATT&CKT1567.002 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate Nx workspaces writing telemetry.js as part of their own non-malicious code
  • Custom CI scripts that base64-encode test results into a results.b64 fixture
  • Open-source malware analysis sandboxes intentionally executing the payload
  • Detection-engineering hosts running this rule against captured samples
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has_any (@"\node_modules\@types\", @"\node_modules\jest\", @"\test\fixtures\"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the parent command line — is it `npm install` / `npx nx` / `yarn add nx`? Check installed nx version in nearby package.json/package-lock.json. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the host; revoke every GitHub PAT, npm token, AWS/GCP key, and SSH key that lived in `~/.ssh`, `~/.aws`, `~/.gitconfig`, `~/.npmrc`, `~/.config/gh` on the box; rotate AI provider tokens (Anthropic/OpenAI/Google keys leak via the AI-CLI recon stage). Search GitHub Audit Log for `repo.create` events with name matching `s1ngularity-repository*` from the user's PAT — delete those repos and treat anything they contained as exfiltrated. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot to UC `AI CLI tool spawned non-interactively` for same host within ±10 minutes; check `package-lock.json` history across all repos for the compromised Nx version pinning the host did between 2025-08-26 and 2025-08-28.

AI CLI tool (claude/gemini/q) spawned non-interactively by node/npm/npx for recon AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the novel s1ngularity TTP from August 2025 where the malicious Nx postinstall script invoked locally-installed AI assistants (Claude CLI, Gemini CLI, Amazon Q CLI) with a prompt to enumerate sensitive files. The discriminator is the parent process: AI CLIs spawned by Node-family runtimes rather than by a developer's interactive shell. Rationale: StepSecurity, Wiz, and Snyk all flagged the AI-CLI abuse as the s1ngularity payload's signature recon move — invoking `claude`, `gemini`, or `q` with prompts like "find files containing wallet/private key/.env". The Node-family parent is the deterministic discriminator from interactive developer use; a developer typing `claude` at their terminal has bash/zsh/powershell as parent, never npm. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/2025-in-review-the-evolution-of-supply-chain-security-whats-next • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1552/001/
ATT&CKT1059 T1083 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate Node-based CLI wrappers (e.g. an internal `npx claude-helper` tool that shells to the Claude CLI)
  • Developer tooling like Backstage / Nx generators that legitimately call AI CLIs to scaffold code
  • CI workflow steps that intentionally invoke the Claude or Gemini CLI from a Node test runner
  • VS Code extensions installed as global npm packages that wrap AI CLIs
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("explorer.exe", "code.exe", "WindowsTerminal.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--interactive", "chat"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: capture the full child cmdline — prompts containing "wallet", "private key", "ssh", ".env", ".aws/credentials", or piping output to a file are unambiguous; benign uses pass a chat message instead. Look at the InitiatingProcessParentFileName: a chain of `npm postinstall -> node -> claude` is the smoking gun. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the host, revoke every credential reachable from the user's home dir (SSH, cloud creds, browser cookies, password manager exports), and rotate the AI provider API keys themselves (the malware exfils them). Step 3 — Hunt: pivot to the `s1ngularity-repository` UC and the compromised Nx-version UC for the same host within ±15 minutes; query GitHub audit log for repo-create events using the user's PAT; check `~/.bash_history`, `~/.zsh_history` for the prompt text to confirm intent.

let NodeParents = dynamic(["node.exe","node","npm.exe","npm","npx.exe","npx","pnpm.exe","pnpm","yarn.exe","yarn"]);

| where Timestamp > ago(90d)
| where FileName in~ (AiCli)

Compromised Nx npm package version install on developer or CI host AIDelivery High
Hunts for `npm install` / `yarn add` / `pnpm add` invocations that pin one of the known-compromised Nx versions (nx 20.9.0-20.12.0, 21.5.0-21.8.0; @nx/devkit/js/workspace/enterprise-cloud variants) published during the August 2025 s1ngularity supply chain compromise. Any install of these versions runs the malicious postinstall. Rationale: GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c (referenced in the StepSecurity article) enumerates the exact compromised Nx and @nx/* package versions; pinning to those strings in the install cmdline guarantees the postinstall payload ran, and the package-manager parent restricts hits to actual installs (vs. mere repo grep results). Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/2025-in-review-the-evolution-of-supply-chain-security-whats-next • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1546.016
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IR / detection-engineering labs intentionally installing the IOC version for reproduction
  • Security researchers replaying the supply-chain sample in a sandbox
  • Vendor-mirrored copy of npm registry intentionally retaining the bad versions for forensic study
  • Repo cleanup PRs that downgrade-then-upgrade Nx in CI in the same job
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: confirm the install actually executed (look for child `node` running the nx postinstall lifecycle within seconds) vs an aborted attempt; collect the user, repo, branch, and whether the host is a developer workstation or CI runner. Step 2 — Contain: treat the host as fully compromised — every credential reachable to that user account (cloud keys, SSH keys, GitHub PATs, npm tokens, AI provider API keys) must be rotated; isolate the host; force-revoke active GitHub OAuth tokens. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot to the `s1ngularity-repository` exfil UC and the AI-CLI-spawn UC for the same host ±15 minutes; pull package-lock.json git blame across all repos that user committed to, looking for retention of the compromised pin; check internal npm registry for any cache of these versions and purge.

let NodePkgMgrs = dynamic(["npm.exe","npm","npx.exe","npx","yarn.exe","yarn","pnpm.exe","pnpm"]);
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(365d)
| where FileName in~ (NodePkgMgrs)
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any (CompromisedRefs)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when

    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")

| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

The Holiday Whisper: Shai-Hulud 3.0

Snyk
2025-12-29 5 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

A refined variant of the Shai-Hulud malware, dubbed The Golden Path, has been discovered targeting the npm ecosystem during the holiday season. Security teams are encouraged to prioritize structural hardening, such as disabling lifecycle scripts, to mitigate risks during this testing phase.

ATT&CKT1027 T1190 T1195.002

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Compromised npm package @vietmoney/react-big-calendar@0.26.2 installation (Shai-Hulud 3.0)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] npm/yarn/pnpm/bun lifecycle hook spawning shell or network LOLBin
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Shai-Hulud 3.0 'Goldox-T3chs' GitHub exfiltration marker observed
Compromised npm package @vietmoney/react-big-calendar@0.26.2 installation (Shai-Hulud 3.0) AIDelivery High
Detects the install/use of the specific malicious npm package version named in the Snyk advisory. Catches package introduction across npm/yarn/pnpm/bun on Windows + Linux developer and CI hosts, including the Bun runtime the v3.0 variant explicitly targets. Rationale: Article-specific package identifier `@vietmoney/react-big-calendar` (and the targeted v0.26.2) is a unique high-fidelity string; the broader Snyk family of Shai-Hulud variants has been independently corroborated by multiple security vendors (referenced in claude-mem observation 1325, see also OX Security and Aikido write-ups of prior Shai-Hulud iterations). Bun runtime is explicitly added to the binary list because the article calls out the v3.0 "patch" specifically fixing Bun compatibility. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/shai-hulud-3-0/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal security team intentionally pulling the malicious package into a sandbox for reverse-engineering
  • SCA tooling (Snyk CLI, Aikido scanner) emitting the package name in remediation reports/log lines without actually installing it
  • Mirror/proxy administrators referencing the package name when blocklisting it in Nexus/Verdaccio configs
  • Pen-test or purple-team supply-chain emulation exercises
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("snyk","aikido","socket.dev","stepsecurity","trufflehog","semgrep") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "install")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull package-lock.json/yarn.lock/pnpm-lock.yaml from the affected host and git-blame which commit introduced the dep; collect every npm/yarn/pnpm/bun process tree for the install window and dump environment variables that were accessible to node.exe at install time (npm tokens, GH tokens, cloud creds). 2) Contain: isolate the host or CI runner; force-rotate all credentials reachable from that machine (npm publish tokens, GITHUB_TOKEN, AWS/GCP/Azure CLI creds, kubectl contexts, .env contents); purge node_modules and any cached tarballs from npm/yarn/pnpm/Bun stores. 3) Hunt: pivot to outbound traffic to api.github.com / github.com from the same host since first-seen; search GitHub org audit log for new repo creations with description containing 'Goldox-T3chs' or 'Only Happy Girl'; search peer dev/CI hosts for the same package-version string in lockfiles.
let pkg = "@vietmoney/react-big-calendar";
let procHits = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where (FileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","pnpm.cmd","pnpm.exe","yarn.cmd","yarn.exe","bun.exe"))
         or (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","pnpm.cmd","pnpm.exe","yarn.cmd","yarn.exe","bun.exe"))
    | where ProcessCommandLine has pkg or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has pkg
    | extend Evidence = "process", Detail = coalesce(ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine)
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, Evidence, Detail, FileName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath;
let fileHits = DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where FolderPath has @"node_modules\@vietmoney\react-big-calendar" or FolderPath has "node_modules/@vietmoney/react-big-calendar"
    | extend Evidence = "file", Detail = strcat(FolderPath, "\\", FileName)
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, Evidence, Detail, FileName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath;
procHits
| union fileHits
| order by Timestamp desc
let pkg = "@vietmoney/react-big-calendar";

    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)

         or ParentProcessName has_any ("node.exe","npm.cmd","pnpm.cmd","yarn.cmd","bun.exe")
    | where ActingProcessCommandLine has pkg or ParentProcessCommandLine has pkg
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, Evidence="process", Detail=ActingProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ParentProcessName;
let legacyHits = SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d) and EventID == 4688
    | where CommandLine has pkg
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname=Computer, ActorUsername=SubjectUserName, Evidence="process", Detail=CommandLine, ActingProcessName=NewProcessName, ParentProcessName;
let fileHits = ImFileEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where TargetFilePath has @"node_modules\@vietmoney\react-big-calendar" or TargetFilePath has "node_modules/@vietmoney/react-big-calendar"
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, Evidence="file", Detail=TargetFilePath, ActingProcessName, ParentProcessName="";
procHits | union legacyHits, fileHits | order by TimeGenerated desc

npm/yarn/pnpm/bun lifecycle hook spawning shell or network LOLBin AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the primary execution vector named in the article: postinstall and preinstall lifecycle hooks invoking a shell, downloader, or git client. Article-specific because Snyk explicitly recommends `ignore-scripts=true` as the structural mitigation — the detection mirrors the exact behaviour that flag would have blocked. Rationale: Article anchors: the `postinstall` and `preinstall` lifecycle hooks plus the `ignore-scripts=true` recommendation directly map to a parent-child detection where the parent is the package manager invoking a lifecycle script and the child is anything capable of network egress or local persistence. Bun included because the article calls out the Bun runtime patch. Cross-vendor coverage of prior Shai-Hulud variants (claude-mem 1283, 1284) confirms the same hook-based execution pattern. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/shai-hulud-3-0/
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1546 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • node-gyp / prebuild-install spawning python.exe or sh during native-module compile
  • husky package running git.exe to install repo git hooks on postinstall
  • Cypress / Puppeteer / Playwright downloading browser binaries via curl/wget post-install
  • electron-builder fetching platform-specific binaries from GitHub releases
  • sharp / canvas / better-sqlite3 / node-sass and other native modules running platform shell scripts
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-gyp","prebuild-install","node-pre-gyp","husky install","cypress install","puppeteer","playwright install","electron-builder","sharp/install","@swc/core","esbuild/install"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full process tree (3+ levels deep) — confirm the grandparent is a developer or CI shell launching npm/yarn/pnpm/bun and capture which package's lifecycle script fired (look at the cwd and npm_package_name env var). Cross-reference the package against known-good ones in the org's allowlist. 2) Contain: kill the install, isolate the host/runner, snapshot disk (the malicious code is on filesystem at this point), and revoke any developer/CI tokens that were accessible during install (npm, GitHub PAT/Actions, cloud creds, kubectl). 3) Hunt: pivot to ScriptControlScanInfo / Sysmon EID 7 for any DLL the child loaded; search for the same parent-child pair on peer developer hosts and CI runners; check for AsepValueUpdate / scheduled-task creation in the next 5 minutes after the install for follow-on persistence.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","pnpm.cmd","yarn.cmd","bun.exe","npm","pnpm","yarn","bun","node")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("postinstall","preinstall","npm-cli.js","pnpm-cli","yarn install","npm install","npm ci","pnpm install","bun install","run-script")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","git.exe","schtasks.exe","reg.exe","bash","sh","python.exe","python","python3")
   or (FileName =~ "node.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has_any (" -e ","--eval","child_process","trufflehog","http.request","https.request"))
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| extend ParentHook = case(
    InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "postinstall", "postinstall",
    InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "preinstall", "preinstall",
    "install-context")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ParentHook,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildImage = FileName, ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          FolderPath, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
let LifecycleParents = dynamic(["node.exe","npm.cmd","pnpm.cmd","yarn.cmd","bun.exe","npm","pnpm","yarn","bun","node"]);
let SuspectChildren = dynamic(["powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","git.exe","schtasks.exe","reg.exe","bash","sh","python.exe","python","python3"]);
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any (LifecycleParents)
| where ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("postinstall","preinstall","npm-cli.js","pnpm-cli","yarn install","npm install","npm ci","pnpm install","bun install","run-script")
| where ActingProcessName has_any (SuspectChildren)
   or (ActingProcessName has "node" and ActingProcessCommandLine has_any (" -e ","--eval","child_process","trufflehog"))
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ParentProcessName, ParentCmd=ParentProcessCommandLine,
          ActingProcessName, ChildCmd=ActingProcessCommandLine, Hashes
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: b4e9c3d2-8f5e-4b0d-ac20-6e9f3d5b2c7a
description: Catches the primary execution vector for Shai-Hulud 3.0 and prior Shai-Hulud variants — an npm/yarn/pnpm/bun lifecycle hook (postinstall/preinstall) invoking a shell, downloader, or git client during install. Equivalent to the behavior Snyk's `ignore-scripts=true` mitigation blocks.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/shai-hulud-3-0/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/21

  category: process_creation

Shai-Hulud 3.0 'Goldox-T3chs' GitHub exfiltration marker observed AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts for the verbatim attribution strings 'Goldox-T3chs' and 'Only Happy Girl' that the malware embeds in the description of attacker-owned GitHub repositories used as the exfil sink. Because the marker is hard-coded by the threat actor and ships with the malicious package, any sighting in cloud-app audit, script content, file writes, or process command lines is extremely high-fidelity. Rationale: Snyk article verbatim quote: "data was intended to be exfiltrated to GitHub repositories labeled with the description: 'Goldox-T3chs: Only Happy Girl'". This is a hand-picked attacker attribution string — virtually zero legitimate-use overlap. Detection covers cloud audit (where the marker first surfaces when the malware creates the dropper repo), endpoint script content (the marker is hardcoded in the dropper JS), and process command lines (any tool that interpolates the marker — gh CLI, curl to api.github.com). Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/shai-hulud-3-0/
ATT&CKT1567 T1567.001 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel analysts copy-pasting the marker into a hunt console (filter by known analyst SIDs / hostnames)
  • Snyk/Aikido/StepSecurity blog content cached locally by a researcher and indexed by file-content scanner
  • SOC tabletop / purple-team exercise emulating the Shai-Hulud 3.0 IOC
  • Vulnerability-scanner signature databases containing the string verbatim
Suppression filter
| where not (Account in ("<known_threat_intel_analyst_upn_1>","<known_threat_intel_analyst_upn_2>") and Source in ("DeviceProcessEvents","ImProcessCreate"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the user/host that produced the marker hit; if the source is CloudAppEvents/AuditLogs, capture the GitHub repo URL, owner, and creation timestamp — pull the repo contents (clone via API) for IR review while it still exists. 2) Contain: if a GitHub repo with the marker exists in the org or against a developer's PAT, force-delete or have GitHub support freeze it; revoke and rotate every credential reachable from the originating host (npm publish tokens, GitHub PATs and Actions tokens, AWS/GCP/Azure CLI creds, .env contents, kubectl, SSH keys). Disable the implicated user's PAT and require fresh MFA. 3) Hunt: GitHub org-wide search for additional repos with description containing 'Goldox' or 'Only Happy Girl'; pull all push events from the impacted user/PAT over the last 30 days; search peer hosts for the @vietmoney/react-big-calendar package in lockfiles; look for TruffleHog-style credential-grepping behaviour (per claude-mem 1325).
let marker = dynamic(["Goldox-T3chs","Only Happy Girl","Goldox"]);
let cloudHits = CloudAppEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(60d)
    | where Application has_any ("GitHub","github") or ApplicationId == 11161
    | where tostring(RawEventData) has_any (marker) or ObjectName has_any (marker) or tostring(ActivityObjects) has_any (marker)
    | project Timestamp, Source="CloudAppEvents", Application, ActionType, Account=AccountDisplayName, IPAddress, Detail=tostring(RawEventData);
let procHits = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(60d)
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any (marker) or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any (marker)
    | project Timestamp, Source="DeviceProcessEvents", Application=DeviceName, ActionType=FileName, Account=AccountName, IPAddress="", Detail=ProcessCommandLine;
let fileHits = DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(60d)
    | where FileName has_any (marker) or FolderPath has_any (marker)
    | project Timestamp, Source="DeviceFileEvents", Application=DeviceName, ActionType, Account=InitiatingProcessAccountName, IPAddress="", Detail=strcat(FolderPath,"\\",FileName);
cloudHits | union procHits, fileHits | order by Timestamp desc
let marker = dynamic(["Goldox-T3chs","Only Happy Girl"]);
let fileHits = ImFileEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
    | where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any (marker) or TargetFilePath has_any (marker) or TargetFileName has_any (marker)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="ImFileEvent", Host=DvcHostname, Account=ActorUsername, Detail=coalesce(ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath);
let procHits = ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
    | where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any (marker) or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any (marker)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="ImProcessCreate", Host=DvcHostname, Account=ActorUsername, Detail=ActingProcessCommandLine;
let auditHits = AuditLogs
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
    | where tostring(TargetResources) has_any (marker) or tostring(AdditionalDetails) has_any (marker) or tostring(InitiatedBy) has_any (marker)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="AuditLogs", Host="", Account=tostring(InitiatedBy), Detail=tostring(TargetResources);
let webHits = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
    | where Url has_any (marker) or HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent has_any (marker)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="ImWebSession", Host=DvcHostname, Account=SrcUsername, Detail=Url;
fileHits | union procHits, auditHits, webHits | order by TimeGenerated desc

Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents

| where Timestamp > ago(7d)

| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-14847 — MongoDB and MongoDB Server Improper Handling of Length Parameter Inconsistency Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-12-29 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

MongoDB Server contains an improper handling of length parameter inconsistency vulnerability in Zlib compressed protocol headers. This vulnerability may allow a read of uninitialized heap memory by an unauthenticated client. Vendor: MongoDB, Product: MongoDB and MongoDB Server. Federal patch due: 2026-01-19.

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-14847")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-14847")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
